most of the time, like I’ve had way too much coffee even when I haven’t, tapping my foot in meetings and clenching my shoulders so much that I can’t seem to just sit and breathe without feeling like something’s about to go wrong.\n- S059 (Concentration_Difficulty): During calls or when I’m trying to write a simple automation script, my mind keeps drifting to what I might have forgotten or who I might be letting down, and sometimes it just goes completely blank so I’m staring at the screen with no idea what I was about to say or do.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- I worry a lot about messing up scripts for this big client, saying something wrong when I’m mentoring, my parents’ health, and my student loans—even when nothing specific is actually wrong, my brain jumps to worst‑case scenarios in all those areas.\n\nDoctor: How much are these worries and physical symptoms getting in the way of your daily life—for example at work, in your relationships, or in taking care of yourself—and do they feel distressing enough that you wish they would stop or be much less intense?"

So definitely more than six months, not just a few weeks after one thing.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling mostly sad or down and losing interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy, or is it more that you feel tense and worried but your mood is otherwise okay when you’re not thinking about your worries?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I feel tense and worried all the time rather than mainly sad. I’ve been skipping dance nights and hikes and canceling plans, but that’s mostly because I feel too drained and anxious or stuck thinking about work, not because I don’t like those things anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in this constant worry mode, do you also notice physical symptoms like restlessness, muscle tension, feeling keyed up, trouble concentrating, irritability, or trouble falling or staying asleep—and if so, which of these are happening for you most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely notice several of those most days. I feel really keyed up and tense a lot—my shoulders are tight and I’m always tapping my foot—and I have a hard time falling asleep and staying asleep, so I wake up feeling unrested. My concentration is also pretty bad lately; my mind goes blank or keeps drifting when I’m trying to focus on work.\n\nDoctor: When your worrying starts, do you feel like you can control or put it aside if you really try, or does it feel excessive and very hard to stop even when you know it’s not helpful?\n\nPatient: It feels really excessive and like I can’t stop it, even when I’m telling myself it’s not logical or helpful. Once a worry starts, it just spirals into worst‑case scenarios, and trying to distract myself or reason with it doesn’t really switch it off—it just keeps running in the background.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that peak within minutes—like your heart racing, shortness of breath, or feeling like you might lose control or die—or is your anxiety more of a constant, slow-burning worry throughout the day without those sharp spikes?\n\nPatient: It’s much more of a constant, slow‑burning worry throughout the day. I feel wired and on edge a lot, but I don’t really get those sudden, intense episodes where I think I’m going to die or completely lose control.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mainly focused on work and this promotion, or do you also find yourself excessively worrying about many other areas of life like health, family, finances, or everyday things even when there’s no clear problem?\n\nPatient: It started with work and the promotion, but now it’s spread to pretty much everything. I worry a lot about messing up scripts for this big client, saying something wrong when I’m mentoring, my parents’ health, and my student loans—even when nothing specific is actually wrong, my brain jumps to worst‑case scenarios in all those areas.\n\nDoctor: How much are these worries and physical symptoms getting in the way of your daily life—for example at work, in your relationships, or in taking care of yourself—and do they feel distressing enough that you wish they would stop or be much less intense?\n\nPatient: They’re getting in the way a lot—I’ve started missing project deadlines and zoning out in client meetings because I’m so anxious and unfocused. I cancel plans with friends and skip dance or hikes because I feel too drained and wired, and the poor sleep makes everything harder. It’s really distressing, and I definitely wish the worrying and tension would just stop or at least be way less intense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a strong urge to do certain repetitive actions or mental rituals—like checking, counting, or repeating things—to try to prevent something bad from happening or to neutralize your worries, or is it mostly just the worrying itself without any rituals like that?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly just the worrying itself without specific rituals. My mind runs through worst‑case scenarios and replays emails or possible escalations, but I don’t have set behaviors like counting, checking things in a certain order, or repeating phrases to make the anxiety go away."

I feel wired and on edge a lot, but I don’t really get those sudden, intense episodes where I think I’m going to die or completely lose control.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mainly focused on work and this promotion, or do you also find yourself excessively worrying about many other areas of life like health, family, finances, or everyday things even when there’s no clear problem?\n\nPatient: It started with work and the promotion, but now it’s spread to pretty much everything. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.
